- **low** `como-dice-el-dicho + cada-loco-con-su-tema / NEW WORDS` — every cloud has a silver lining / a bird in the hand is worth two in the bush / don't look a gift horse in the mouth / once bitten, twice shy / speak of the devil → **Use the plain versions already in the sentences as the main gloss; English proverb second, in brackets** _(Word-card meaning is an English proverb the learner may not know; sentences already use plain English.)_
- **low** `practica-me-mori-de-risa / > hacer la gamba, > manija` — That's what friends are for: having your back. / I'm so hyped for the concert. / The guys are really hyped. / I'm so wound up I can't sleep. → **Friends are there to help you out. / I'm so excited about the concert. / The guys are really excited. / I'm so excited I can't sleep.** _('Having your back', 'hyped', 'wound up' are English slang; the tip already glosses manija as 'very excited'.)_
- **low** (unsure) `no-te-lo-tomes-a-mal / > te ofendiste` — Did you take offense at what I said? / Juli said you took offense. / I think you took offense. → **Did what I said offend you? / Juli said you got offended. / I think you got offended.** _('Take offense' is formal English; 'got offended' is plainer and matches how the Spanish sounds.)_
- **low** (unsure) `se-instalaron-en-la-boca + practica-se-instalaron / conventillo` — conventillo = 'tenement house' / 'tenement' in about 15 sentences → **Keep 'conventillo' in the English (like mate and asado) and explain it once in the tip: 'an old shared house where many poor families rented one room each'.** _('Tenement' is a rare English word a B1 non-native reader is unlikely to know.)_
- **low** (unsure) `me-hizo-reir / 9f5da17f` — Esa canción me hace acordar a la primaria. | That song reminds me of grade school. → **That song reminds me of primary school.** _('Grade school' is US-only; 'primary school' is clearer for international readers and closer to 'primaria'.)_
- **low** `ya-sos-de-aca / TIP 8e491aaa` — When your **acento** gives you away less and less → **"When people notice your **acento** less and less"** _('Gives you away' is an idiom a B1 English reader may not know.)_

## es_wrong (40)

- **medium** `practica-nos-gusta / 5e9a1e94` — El tren está lleno, tomamos un taxi. | The train is packed, let's take a taxi. → **El tren está lleno, ¿tomamos un taxi? | The train is packed, should we take a taxi?** _(As a statement it means 'we take a taxi'; the course uses the question form for suggestions.)_
- **medium** `facu-y-laburo / c52ffc22` — ¿De qué es tu laburo? → **Remove, or use "¿De qué laburás?" / "¿Cuál es tu laburo?"** _(Nobody asks it this way; the tip itself teaches "¿De qué laburás?".)_
- **medium** (unsure) `queres-podes-vas / 600ff25d` — ¿Adónde sale Ana el sábado? → **¿Adónde va Ana el sábado? (or: ¿Con quién sale Ana el sábado?)** _('Adónde sale' with a named person sounds off; a porteño says 'adónde va' or 'con quién sale'.)_
- **medium** `llegue-tarde / 8964971a` — No te encontré en la parada, ¿ya saliste? → **No te encontré en la parada, ¿ya te fuiste?** _(Leaving the place you were at is 'irse'; 'saliste' means leaving home.)_
- **medium** `practica-la-sobremesa / d44efefa` — ¿Cuánto tardó el viaje? → **¿Cuánto duró el viaje? (or: ¿Cuánto tardaron en llegar?)** _(A trip 'dura'; people or vehicles 'tardan'.)_
- **medium** `se-recibio / 8240d010` — El casamiento por civil tardó diez minutos. → **El civil duró diez minutos.** _(An event 'dura', it does not 'tarda'; and people call the ceremony 'el civil'.)_
- **medium** `me-siento-mal / d58abf4f` — ¿Me comprás las pastillas cuando vas a la farmacia? → **¿Me comprás las pastillas si vas a la farmacia?** _(For a future action Spanish needs 'cuando vayas'; 'si vas' avoids the untaught subjunctive.)_
- **medium** `sos-un-genio / c0bc98b2` — No sabés cuánto te agradezco. → **No sabés cuánto te lo agradezco.** _(Agradecer needs its object here; without "lo" it sounds cut off.)_
- **medium** `puede-ser-que / cccdc5d8` — Llegamos temprano, no creo que abran todavía. | We're early, I don't think they're open yet. → **Llegamos temprano, no creo que estén abiertos todavía.** _("Que abran todavía" does not mean "are open yet"; Spanish and English do not match.)_
- **medium** `como-no-dona-rosa / 9532e835` — —Perdón, no pedí permiso. —Faltaba más, querido. → **—Perdón, no pedí permiso. —No es nada, querido.** _('Faltaba más' answers thanks or a request, not an apology; English 'don't mention it' is also wrong here.)_
- **medium** `costumbres / TIP c7048a9e` — si te dicen a las nueve, llegás nueve y media → **si te dicen a las nueve, llegás a las nueve y media** _(Missing "a las"; nobody says "llegás nueve y media".)_
- **medium** `llevo-un-ano-aprendiendo / 3ce22e95` — Cami y Santi llevan un mes sin hablar. → **Cami y Santi llevan un mes sin hablarse. (or: Hace un mes que Cami y Santi no se hablan.)** _("Sin hablar" means silent; not talking to each other needs "hablarse".)_
- **medium** `si-hubieramos-salido / a93dfb17` — Me dormí todo el vuelo. | I slept the whole flight. → **Dormí todo el vuelo. | I slept the whole flight.** _('Me dormí' means 'I fell asleep'; with 'todo el vuelo' it has to be 'dormí'.)_
- **low** `encantado / 677fdf56` — Entiendo poco, ¿repetís más despacio? → **Entiendo poco. ¿Me repetís más despacio?** _(Without 'me' it sounds cut off; every other line uses '¿Me repetís?'.)_
- **low** (unsure) `cuantos-anos-tenes / 5ba58186` — ¿Cuántos años tenés? Tengo diecinueve, pero ella, veinte. → **¿Cuántos años tenés? Tengo diecinueve, y ella, veinte.** _('pero ella, veinte' is awkward; there is no contrast, plain 'y' is what people say.)_
- **low** (unsure) `esta-cerrado / 97415a36` — Mi hija está sucia de helado. | My daughter has ice cream all over her. → **Mi hija está toda sucia. | My daughter is all dirty.  (or: está sucia, tiene helado en la cara)** _('Sucia de helado' is unusual; natives say 'toda sucia' or 'manchada con helado'.)_
- **low** (unsure) `mate-y-facturas / 36c79fbc` — El café de acá está rico, pero el mate no. → **El café de acá es rico, pero las medialunas no.** _(General quality takes 'es rico'; and a café does not serve mate.)_
- **low** (unsure) `ayuda / 6f679bc6` — ¿Dónde es el accidente? → **¿Dónde fue el accidente?  (if past must be avoided: ¿Dónde están?)** _(An accident has already happened; a native asks 'dónde fue'.)_
- **low** `practica-el-clima / 92e825c3` — ¿Hace calor? Sí, y mucha humedad. → **¿Hace calor? Sí, y hay mucha humedad.** _(Without a verb it reads as 'hace humedad', which the unit's own tip says is wrong.)_
- **low** (unsure) `el-cumple / faa8cb8e` — Pablo no está, está jugando en la cancha. | he's out playing on the field → **Pablo no está, está jugando al fútbol. | Pablo's not here, he's playing soccer.** _('Jugando en la cancha' without saying what is unnatural.)_
- **low** `acampamos-en-el-sur / 0327043e` — Acampamos entre dos lagos, al lado de la cordillera. → **Acampamos entre dos lagos, al pie de la cordillera.** _(You can't be 'next to' a mountain range; the phrase is 'al pie de'.)_
- **low** `te-convido-un-mate / c74280e8` — Mi vecino es tan matero que siempre tiene el termo. → **Mi vecino es tan matero que anda siempre con el termo.** _("Siempre tiene el termo" sounds unfinished; the set phrase is "andar con el termo".)_
- **low** (unsure) `que-te-mejores / d785c9e7` — Ana, ¡que los cumplas feliz y la pases bárbaro! → **Ana, ¡que los cumplas feliz y que la pases bárbaro!** _(The second wish needs its own "que" to sound natural.)_
- **low** (unsure) `que-significa / 28cd1b5e` — Pablo tampoco sabía qué significa esa palabra. → **Pablo tampoco sabía qué significaba esa palabra.** _(After 'sabía' the natural tense is 'significaba'.)_
- **low** `acabo-de / 9671f4ea` — En invierno suelo tomar un café con leche. | …a coffee with milk. → **En invierno suelo tomar café con leche. | In winter I usually drink café con leche.** _("Un" is odd for a habit; "coffee with milk" is clumsy English.)_
- **low** `no-me-alcanza / 007a7cba` — ¿Las tarifas de agua también aumentaron? → **¿Las tarifas del agua también aumentaron?** _(Needs the article: 'del agua', like 'del gas' elsewhere.)_
- **low** (unsure) `cualquier-cosa-avisame aa8fad00` — Estimado Juan: desde ya, gracias por la entrevista. | Dear Juan: thanks in advance for the interview. → **Estimado Juan: gracias por la entrevista de ayer. (or 'desde ya, gracias por tu tiempo')** _('Desde ya' thanks for something still to come; you do not thank in advance for an interview already given.)_
- **low** (unsure) `se-merece-el-ascenso / b845628c` — No le podemos exigir más a Mati sin ascenso. → **No le podemos exigir más a Mati si no lo ascienden. (or: ...sin darle el ascenso.)** _('sin ascenso' is clipped and unnatural here.)_
- **low** (unsure) `resulta-que / 673d5b77` — Resultó mentira. → **Resultó ser mentira. (or: Al final era mentira.)** _(Bare 'resultó mentira' sounds clipped; people add 'ser' or say 'era mentira'.)_
- **low** `segun-el-diario / 3cad4a3c` — No, todavía no confirmó nadie. → **No, todavía no lo confirmó nadie. | No, nobody has confirmed it yet.** _("Confirmar" needs its object here; without "lo" it sounds cut off.)_
- **low** `lo-lindo-de-la-ciudad / aa7f47a0` — Con el costo de vida no puedo ahorrar. | With the cost of living I can't save. → **Con este costo de vida no puedo ahorrar. | With this cost of living I can't save.** _('Con el costo de vida' alone says nothing; needs 'este' (as in 28d847bc).)_
- **low** `si-hubiera-ahorrado / a488b278` — Si hubiéramos salido temprano, estaríamos en casa. → **Si hubiéramos salido temprano, ya estaríamos en casa. | … we would be home by now.** _(Without 'ya' the sentence sounds incomplete; the tip itself uses 'ya estaríamos'.)_
- **low** `tendria-que-haber / d5a38e16` — Tendrías que haber ido a aprovechar los precios. | You should have gone to take advantage of the prices. → **Tendrías que haber aprovechado los precios. | You should have taken advantage of the prices.** _('Ir a aprovechar los precios' is clumsy; nobody phrases it that way.)_
- **low** (unsure) `tendria-que-haber / 7bd3dd76, e7b3e03e` — No aproveché y me arrepiento. / No aproveché, tendría que haber ido. → **No lo aproveché y me arrepiento. / No aproveché la oportunidad, tendría que haber ido.** _('Aprovechar' needs an object here; bare 'no aproveché' sounds cut off.)_
- **low** `tendria-que-haber / c7eafa5c` — No aproveché a mi abuelo, tendría que haber ido más. | … I should have gone more. → **No aproveché a mi abuelo, tendría que haberlo visitado más. | … I should have visited him more.** _('Haber ido más' has no destination; unclear in both languages.)_
- **low** `practica-cada-loco-con-su-tema / 5d1c52bc` — ¿En el fondo, estás contento? → **En el fondo, ¿estás contento?** _(The opening question mark goes where the question starts.)_
- **low** (unsure) `practica-me-mori-de-risa / d8e50a52, 9021a8f3` — Gracias por hacer la gamba siempre. | Thanks for always being there for me. / ¿Me venís a hacer la gamba al partido? | Are you coming to the game to cheer me on? → **Gracias por hacerme la gamba siempre. / ¿Me hacés la gamba y venís conmigo al partido? | Will you do me a favor and come to the game with me?** _(The phrase needs the person (hacerme la gamba); it means keep company or help out, not cheer on.)_
- **low** (unsure) `practica-se-instalaron / 6e38e827` — Cuando éramos mochileros, hicimos dedo por todo el sur. → **Cuando éramos mochileros, hacíamos dedo por todo el sur. (or: Ese verano hicimos dedo por todo el sur.)** _(A habit inside a 'cuando éramos' frame takes the imperfect.)_
- **low** (unsure) `ya-sos-de-aca / 080eaa38` — Quiero lograr un acento porteño. → **"Quiero tener acento porteño." or "Quiero hablar con acento porteño."** _(Nobody says 'lograr un acento'; you have or speak with an accent.)_
- **low** `ya-sos-de-aca / cc0a4a73` — Pude lograr todo esto gracias a mis amigos. → **"Logré todo esto gracias a mis amigos." | "I achieved all this thanks to my friends."** _('Pude lograr' is redundant (managed to manage).)_

## tip_wrong (33)

- **high** `quien-sera / > estarás vs TIP ce15b13a` — Tip: 'the statement is **debés estar cansado**'; drills: Estarás cansado después del viaje. / Estarás nervioso por la mudanza. / Estarás contenta, ganó tu equipo. → **Change the five 'estarás' statements to 'Debés estar cansado después del viaje.' etc., and keep estarás only in questions (¿Estarás cansado?).** _(Tip and drills contradict each other; 'Estarás cansado' as a statement sounds bookish in Buenos Aires.)_
- **high** `me-gustaria / 52d95064` — Me gustarían dos entradas. | I'd like two tickets. → **Remove, or change to a wish: 'Me gustarían unas vacaciones largas.'** _(The unit's own tip says you order with 'quería dos entradas', not 'me gustaría'.)_
- **medium** `otro-cafe / TIP 25308c48 vs dc8b24e3, 1b4fe546, 0f1bb2ca` — Tip: 'In a café nobody orders one slice' — but: 'Una tostada, por favor.' / 'Otra tostada, por favor.' / 'Un licuado, una tostada y un agua, por favor.' → **Change the ordering lines to 'tostadas' ('Tostadas, por favor', 'Más tostadas' later) and keep 'tostada' only for home offers ('¿Otra tostada?').** _(The sentences do exactly what the tip says nobody does.)_
- **medium** `la-familia / NEW WORDS novia` — novia=girlfriend [Also used for a fiancé and for the groom at a wedding.] → **novia: [Also used for a fiancée and for the bride at a wedding.]** _(The note for 'novio' was copied onto 'novia'; it says groom instead of bride.)_
- **medium** `el-finde / TIP 318d0b7a vs TIP 4ff7f7af` — Tell the story in order with **primero**, **entonces**, **al final**. → **In TIP 318d0b7a change to: Tell the story in order with **primero**, **después**, **al final**. (and gloss entonces as "so, then" in NEW WORDS)** _(The two tips in the same unit contradict each other; the second says entonces is mostly 'so'.)_
- **medium** `te-llame / TIP 89462fe8 vs > recibí, > recibiste` — "a message is not *received*, it *reaches* you: ¿Te llegó mi audio?" — but the unit drills "Recibí tu mensaje.", "Recibí tu audio, pero no entendí nada.", "Recibí un audio de Mati.", "¿Recibiste el mensaje que te mandé?" → **Keep recibí/recibiste for paquete, plata, mail, regalo. Change message/audio lines to llegar: "Me llegó tu mensaje.", "Me llegó tu audio, pero no entendí nada.", "Me llegó un audio de Mati.", "¿Te llegó el mensaje que te mandé?" and add "me llegó / te llegó" to NEW WORDS. Or soften the tip to "porteños more often say...".** _(The tip says messages are not 'received', then ten sentences say exactly that.)_
- **medium** `cuando-era-chico / TIP da883151 vs sentences` — Tip: "Porteños mostly say colegio for school" — but the unit says escuela ~18 times ("¿A qué escuela ibas?", "Fede iba a mi escuela.", "iba a la escuela solo") and colegio 5 times. → **Switch most lines to colegio: "¿Juli iba a tu colegio?", "Fede iba a mi colegio.", "Éramos compañeros en el colegio." Keep 2–3 with escuela.** _(The sentences do the opposite of what the tip tells the learner.)_
- **medium** `de-que-cuadro-sos + socio-del-club (words: hincho, hinchás, hinchamos, hinchaba) vs TIP bb6b163b / 221c8d0b` — Tip: "Hinchar por is for one game or the national team" and "day to day, people simply say somos del mismo cuadro" — but sentences: Hincho por un cuadro de Rosario, como mi abuelo. / Hincho por un cuadro que nunca ganó nada. / ¿Hinchás por un cuadro de Córdoba? / De chico hinchaba por otro cuadro. / Hinchamos por el club del barrio. → **For a club use ser (hincha) de: Soy de un cuadro de Rosario, como mi abuelo. / Soy hincha de un cuadro que nunca ganó nada. / ¿Sos de un cuadro de Córdoba? / De chico era de otro cuadro. / Somos del club del barrio. Keep hinchar por only for one game / the national team.** _(About 12 sentences break the rule the tip gives; a porteño says "soy de", not "hincho por", for his club.)_
- **medium** `las-figuritas / 49d3ebdf, ae725888` — ¡No me acordaba de que vivías acá! / no me acordaba de que hoy era el partido → **¡No me acordaba que vivías acá! / Che, no me acordaba que hoy era el partido.** _(Tip in this unit says people drop 'de' before 'que'; both examples keep it and sound bookish.)_
- **medium** `un-ratito / TIP 9c22a7d9 vs a5c690ed, 37ccbbca, 09199780` — Tip: 'hablá más despacito usually means keep your voice down, not slow down' / a5c690ed '¿Podés hablar más despacito?' = Can you speak more slowly? → **Tip: 'hablá más despacito can mean slower OR quieter — context tells.' Keep d32f4073 as the 'quiet' example.** _(Tip says one thing; three drill sentences in the same group translate it the other way.)_
- **medium** `se-recibio / 1dd92014` — ¿Se murió tu tía? Te acompaño en el sentimiento. → **¿Falleció tu tía? Te acompaño en el sentimiento.** _(The tip says you say 'falleció' to the family; this sentence does the opposite.)_
- **medium** `que-bueno / TIP 295c40a8` — News about people is **novedades** ... *Noticias* is what is on TV. → **Both work for people: ¿hay novedades de Juan? / ¿hay noticias de Juan? 'Las noticias' also means the news on TV. 'Una noticia' is one piece of news.** _(The unit's own sentences say '¿Hay noticias de Diego?' and 'Avisame apenas tengas noticias'. Tip contradicts them.)_
- **medium** `usted / 850a7f18` — Señor, quisiera pedir algo más. | Sir, I'd like to order something else. → **Delete, or: 'Disculpá, te quería pedir algo más.'** _(The tip says nobody orders food with 'quisiera'; and nobody calls the waiter 'Señor'.)_
- **medium** `practica-tenes-razon / 73e8c4e8, b899347f` — Opino que el árbitro estuvo mal. / Opino que el arquero fue el mejor. → **Change to a natural 'opino' use, e.g. "¿Querés saber qué opino? El árbitro estuvo mal." / "Yo opino igual: el arquero fue el mejor." (or move them under 'para mí')** _(The unit's own tip says 'opino que' is TV-panel talk, then drills it twice.)_
- **medium** `que-susto / > corrí (6b6d66a5, dec16213, 40d0f413 …)` — Escuché un ruido y corrí. / Me asusté y corrí. / Vi al ladrón y corrí. → **Use 'salí corriendo' in these three ("Me asusté y salí corriendo"); keep 'corrí' only with a destination (corrí hasta casa, corrí a la pieza).** _(Tip says bare 'corrí' is less natural, yet seven sentences drill it.)_
- **medium** `se-tapo-la-pileta / ca2021cf vs TIP 5c59e9dc` — Se trabó el ascensor. | The elevator got stuck. → **Change to "Se quedó el ascensor." (or "Se trabó la puerta del ascensor.")** _(Tip says a stuck elevator is 'se quedó', not 'se trabó'; sentence contradicts it.)_
- **medium** `no-sabes-lo-que-me-contaron / TIP 026bd560` — In real gossip nobody says *lo engañó*: it is **le metió los cuernos** → **People say **la engañó**, and in gossip even more often **le metió los cuernos**** _(False: "la engañó" is common, and the unit itself drills it in 8 sentences.)_
- **medium** `migraciones / 63b2934b vs TIP 0a7f761b` — Tip: 'For a person or a bus porteños say tardar… el bondi tardó un montón'  but sentence: El colectivo demoró y llegué tarde al turno. → **El colectivo tardó un montón y llegué tarde al turno. | The bus took forever and I was late for my appointment.** _(The sentence breaks the rule the unit's own tip gives.)_
- **medium** `caiste / NEW WORDS caíste note` — caíste=you believed it (you were tricked) (caer) [Me cae bien Juan = I like Juan (as a person). Works like gustar…] → **Replace the note with: [Caer = to fall for a joke or a lie: ¡caíste! = got you!]** _(The note explains 'caer bien', a different meaning, on the card for 'falling for a joke'.)_
- **medium** `me-la-jugue / TIP 64ddc740` — Two more with the *la* inside: **a las apuradas** — in a rush — and **a la larga** — in the long run. → **Teach these two as plain fixed phrases in their own tip, without linking them to the idiomatic 'la'.** _(In these phrases 'la/las' is a normal article, not the object pronoun the unit teaches.)_
- **medium** `paro-docente / TIP ee61df72 vs sentences 7cfd39e2, 2ec8560c, 75dbfac2, 123b6b86, 0db8df7c, 5163085e, 1fdda350` — Tip: "the fixed phrase is levantaron el paro; suspender is for classes, an exam, a march or a service" — but 8 drill sentences say "Suspendieron el paro…", "El paro está suspendido" → **Either change those sentences to "Levantaron el paro…" / "Se levantó el paro", or soften the tip: "levantaron el paro (also: suspendieron el paro, when it is put off)".** _(The tip tells the learner not to say what the unit then drills eight times.)_
- **medium** `gracias-por-todo / TIP 36794004` — Looking back is the preterite: **al principio me costó** → **"Looking back: one finished thing takes the preterite (**me costó**, **me adapté**); how things were takes the imperfect (**al principio era raro**, **no sabía pedir un café**)."** _(Unit's own sentences use the imperfect (era raro, no sabía); the tip says preterite only.)_
- **low** `practica-hola / 24da847b, ed842639, 2ff4357a` — Disculpá, otra tostada y un té, por favor. / Sí, una tostada y un licuado, gracias. → **Use 'tostadas' in café orders.** _(Same clash with the tostadas tip, repeated in the practice unit.)_
- **low** (unsure) `ropa-y-colores / TIP 53370f9c` — **rosa** and **naranja** never change: *una remera rosa*, *un pantalón naranja*. → **"rosa and naranja keep the -a for masculine too: un pantalón rosa. Plural just adds -s: remeras rosas."** _('Never change' is too strong; people say 'remeras rosas / naranjas' all the time.)_
- **low** `comi-y-sali / NEW WORDS conocer` — conocer=to see, to get to know → **conocer=to know (a person or place), to meet** _('To see' as first meaning is misleading; main meanings are know/meet.)_
- **low** `la-parrillada / TIP d9075515` — **vuelta y vuelta** is barely warm → ****vuelta y vuelta** — seared on each side, almost raw inside** _('Barely warm' is misleading; the NEW WORDS gloss says 'seared, very rare'.)_
- **low** `no-tengo-senal / TIP 6d7c070b` — Day to day people say **bajá** or **bajate** for *download* … *Descargá* is what the screen says. → **"*Descargar* is what the screen says." - **low** `laburo-nuevo / TIP 9cfec53b` — *Conseguir* is the porteño verb for getting anything that took effort → ***Conseguir* is the verb for getting anything that took effort** _(Conseguir is standard Spanish everywhere, not a porteño word.)_
- **low** `quiero-que-vengas / NEW WORDS vayamos` — vayamos=let's go, that we go → **vayamos=that we go** _("Let's go" in Argentina is "vamos"; "vayamos" as let's go is rare and misleading.)_
- **low** `me-dan-asco / > salté (69b4ba50, 68eff3bd)` — Me asusté y salté. / —¿Te asustaste? —Sí, hasta salté. → **"Me asusté y pegué un salto." / "—¿Te asustaste? —Sí, pegué un salto." - **low** (unsure) `puteadas / TIP da8de45b` — **Pelotudo** is stronger than *boludo* and rarely friendly. → ****Pelotudo** is stronger than *boludo*. Close friends do say it as a joke, but from a stranger it is an insult.** _(Friends use 'pelotudo' affectionately all the time; 'rarely friendly' will confuse the learner.)_
- **low** `caiste / TIP a3dfd806` — **se calentó** … and **se ofendió** … are the worst things to say about someone at an asado → **If someone **se calentó** (got mad) or **se ofendió** (took offense), the joke went too far.** _(Odd, unclear claim; the plain meaning is easier and more accurate.)_
- **low** `me-hace-ruido / TIP 0904d601` — The same shape as me cayó la ficha — the feeling happens to you: me quedé helado … me dejó pensando … me partió el alma → **'Something does it to you: me dejó pensando, me partió el alma. And you end up frozen: me quedé helado / helada (here "I" is the subject, so it changes with you: nos quedamos helados).'** _('Me quedé helado' is not the same shape; the subject is 'I', and it agrees in gender and number.)_

## fact_wrong (25)

- **medium** `alfajores-y-chicles / monedas group; cuanto-sale 31504eab, 42584bf6, a020a885; donde-estan-las-llaves ab06ef3c` — ¿Tenés cuatrocientos pesos? No tengo monedas. / ¿Quinientos pesos? Sí, tengo monedas. / Sale mil doscientos, ¿tenés monedas? / Tengo la billetera, pero no tengo monedas. → **Use 'efectivo', 'plata' or 'cambio': '¿Quinientos pesos? Sí, tengo efectivo.' / 'Sale mil doscientos, ¿tenés cambio?' / 'Tengo la billetera, pero no tengo efectivo.' Keep 'moneda' to 2-3 sentences.** _(Coins are almost out of use in Argentina; nobody pays 500 or 1.200 pesos with coins.)_
- **medium** `en-febrero / TIP f30ace7e` — *hace treinta y cinco grados* is a normal January day ... *35 grados* is very hot (95°F) → ***hace treinta grados* is a normal January day ... *35 grados* is very hot (95°F)** _(Average January high in Buenos Aires is about 30°C; the tip also contradicts itself.)_
- **medium** `las-fiestas / > ñoquis (2f740bb8, 8a4e219a, ac582a31, e704dd56)` — En Nochebuena comemos ñoquis. / Mi abuela hace ñoquis para toda la familia en Nochebuena. / Para Navidad hago ñoquis. / El feriado de Navidad cociné ñoquis… → **El 29 comemos ñoquis. / Mi abuela hace ñoquis todos los 29. / ¿Hoy es 29? ¡Ñoquis! / Puse plata abajo del plato.** _(Ñoquis are the 29th tradition, not Christmas food; four sentences teach a wrong picture and none drills 'el 29'.)_
- **medium** (unsure) `me-afanaron / d499ca77` — Dos mil pesos un café: me afanaron. | Two thousand pesos for a coffee: they overcharged me. → **Diez mil pesos un café: me afanaron. (or avoid a number: "¿Tanto por un café? Me afanaron.")** _(A café in Buenos Aires already costs more than 2,000 pesos; the example now reads as cheap, not a rip-off.)_
- **medium** `zona-norte / unit summary` — summary: Belgrano queda en zona norte → **San Isidro queda en zona norte** _(The unit's tip defines zona norte as the suburbs past General Paz. Belgrano is a barrio of the Capital.)_
- **medium** `el-cuarto-oscuro / TIP 60436255 vs 10d0c87e, 1c9a49e6, ed2ecf32, da95098b, 71ce011e, gloss of cuarto oscuro` — Tip: "since 2025 you get one boleta at your mesa" — but sentences: "¿Llevás la boleta de tu casa?", "En el cuarto oscuro no encontré la boleta.", "No encuentro mi boleta.", "Agarrá una boleta.", "Esa boleta no."; gloss: "the closed classroom where you choose your ballot alone" → **Remove or rewrite the old party-ballot lines: "Te dan la boleta en la mesa.", "Marcá la boleta y doblala.", "Esta boleta está mal marcada." Update the gloss: "where you mark your ballot alone".** _(Half the sentences describe the old party-ballot system that the tip says no longer exists nationally.)_
- **medium** `practica-se-instalaron / 58da87fd` — Mientras hierve la pava, busco la yerba. | While the kettle boils, I'll get the yerba. → **Mientras se calienta la pava, busco la yerba. | While the kettle heats up, I'll get the yerba.** _(The same unit's tip says 'no hiervas el agua'; water for mate must not boil.)_
- **low** (unsure) `en-el-kiosco / TIP 81f0edea` — sells drinks, sweets and bus cards ... Many never close. → **sells drinks, sweets and cigarettes, and tops up your SUBE (transport card) ... Many are open 24 hours.** _(The transport card has a name every visitor needs (SUBE); 'never close' overstates it.)_
- **low** (unsure) `tomar-el-bondi / TIP f8d95867` — You tap a **SUBE** card on the reader — on buses, the subte and trains. → **Add: "On the subte and many buses you can now also tap a bank card or your phone."** _(Since 2024–25 contactless cards work on the subte and many lines; SUBE-only is outdated.)_
- **low** (unsure) `me-cobras / 7176d96d` — El depto cuesta ochenta millones. → **El auto cuesta treinta millones.** _(Apartments in Argentina are priced in dollars, not pesos.)_
- **low** (unsure) `pasame-tu-numero / TIP a807dff0` — For a local number you buy a chip — a SIM card — at a phone shop or a kiosco → **Add: 'you will need your passport to activate it'.** _(Prepaid lines must be registered with ID; a learner will be asked for it.)_
- **low** (unsure) `en-la-primaria / TIP a4adbfa9` — In a state school everyone wears a white **guardapolvo** over their clothes → **In a state **primary** school everyone wears a white **guardapolvo**...** _(The white smock is a primaria thing; state secondary students mostly do not wear it.)_
- **low** (unsure) `me-robaron / TIP 65cb8068` — the denuncia is for the insurance and the new SIM → **"the denuncia is for the insurance. To block the line and the phone you call your phone company (*910)."** _(A new SIM/line block is done with the carrier; a police report is not normally required for it.)_
- **low** (unsure) `se-recibio / NEW WORDS por civil note` — The legal wedding is always at the Registro Civil → **The legal wedding is the civil one, usually at the Registro Civil** _(Civil ceremonies can also be held outside the registry office (registrar comes to the venue).)_
- **low** (unsure) `te-aconsejo / da4b1c40` — Es mejor que cambies plata en el banco. | It's better to change money at the bank. → **Es mejor que cambies plata en una casa de cambio. | It's better to change money at an exchange office.** _(Tourists rarely change money at Argentine banks; banks often will not serve non-customers.)_
- **low** (unsure) `la-ruta / 6ea8e81f` — Pagué el peaje con tarjeta. → **Pagué el peaje con el TelePASE. (and one tip line: most tolls are automatic, TelePASE)** _(Buenos Aires expressway tolls no longer take cash or card at the booth; TelePASE is what people say.)_
- **low** (unsure) `en-cuotas + a-medias + usted / peso amounts` — Con mil pesos no me alcanza. / ¿Cuánto gastaste? Poco, dos mil pesos. / Prestame mil pesos / Te debo mil pesos (tip) / ¿No tendrás cambio de mil? → **Raise to current sizes (diez mil, veinte mil, cincuenta mil) or avoid numbers: 'Prestame algo de plata, que no me alcanza.'** _(A thousand pesos buys almost nothing now; a coffee costs several thousand. Other lines already use 20-60 thousand.)_
- **low** (unsure) `zona-norte / TIP ef2f7720` — Zona norte ... has the money and the árboles; zona sur ... is cheaper. → **"Zona norte has the reputation for money and trees; zona sur is, in general, cheaper" (zona sur also has leafy, well-off places like Adrogué or Lomas).** _(Stated as fact, it is a stereotype; zona sur has rich leafy towns too.)_
- **low** (unsure) `se-aplaude-al-asador / 3d8a4cba` — Acá se brinda mirando a los ojos. → **Replace with a safer custom: "Acá se brinda antes de empezar a comer."** _(Eye-contact toasting is not a clear Argentine rule; reads like a German/French custom.)_
- **low** (unsure) `migraciones / d5cac53a` — Con la precaria ya podés abrir una cuenta. | With the temporary residence permit you can already open an account. → **Con la precaria ya podés laburar. / Con la precaria ya podés sacar el CUIL.** _(Most banks ask for a DNI; opening an account with only the precaria is often refused.)_
- **low** (unsure) `con-tal-de-que / c256926f + se-alquila / eb7c950f + estoy-al-horno / d20cbeb7` — Me alcanza con que me prestes mil pesos. / Vendí mi bici por cincuenta mil pesos. / ¿Dos pesos de propina? → **Use amounts that make sense today or avoid numbers: ...me prestes diez mil pesos. / Vendí mi bici por trescientos mil pesos. / ¿Cien pesos de propina?** _(Peso amounts are out of date: two pesos is nothing, 50,000 is far too cheap for a bike.)_
- **low** `las-elecciones / NEW WORDS veda + TIP 67c1e923` — "from the night before the vote: no campaigning and no alcohol sold" → **"veda: campaigning stops 48 hours before the vote (Friday morning); from the night before, no alcohol is sold."** _(The campaign ban starts 48 hours before, not the night before; only the alcohol ban starts Saturday night.)_
- **low** (unsure) `como-dice-el-dicho / TIP e6b7166a` — Two you'll hear every week in Buenos Aires: sobre gustos no hay nada escrito; el que quiere celeste, que le cueste → **Two more you'll hear in Buenos Aires: …** _('Every week' is an overstatement; these are known but not that frequent.)_
- **low** (unsure) `practica-me-hace-ruido / 6050601b` — La empresa dice que el aguinaldo viene en dos cuotas. | The company says the extra month's pay comes in two installments. → **La empresa dice que este mes paga el aguinaldo en cuotas. | The company says it's paying this month's bonus in installments.** _(By law the aguinaldo is always two payments (June and December), as the neighbor sentence says; this reads as news.)_
- **low** (unsure) `se-instalaron-en-la-boca / 6c818f7f; mis-abuelos-italianos / TIP a5efa082` — Casi todos en Buenos Aires tienen raíces en Europa. / 'Most porteños have an abuelo or bisabuelo who llegó en barco from Italy or Spain.' → **Muchísima gente en Buenos Aires tiene raíces en Europa. / 'A great many porteños have…'** _(Overstated; many porteños have indigenous, provincial, Paraguayan, Bolivian or Middle Eastern roots.)_

## Reviewers overall notes

- c01: Spanish is clean and properly porteño: voseo, che, dale, joya, posta, un gusto, zona norte/Capital are all right; I found almost no real grammar errors in these 18 units.
- c01: Pacing is very uneven: units of 8 and 11 sentences (vos-y-sos, de-donde-sos) next to units with 22–26 new words and 100+ sentences (encantado, de-todos-lados).
- c01: Template filler is the main quality problem: 'Mi X es de Y, pero mi Z no', '¿sos [nationality] o de [city]?', '¿Comida o un licuado?' — correct Spanish with no real situation behind it.
- c01: The name swap left marks: Argentine nicknames on Germans/Australians/grandparents, and names added to questions where the speaker clearly does not know the person.
- c01: Several listed words are never practised (Argentina, Canadá, Francia, Alemania, México, España, Italia, mis viejos), and the tostadas tip is contradicted by its own sentences.
- c01: English is plain and faithful overall; only small issues ('let me through' for permiso, 'sorted', 'buys you patience', 'father-in-law' as a form of address).
- c02: Spanish is clean and natural porteño almost everywhere; I found no real grammar errors and very few English mistakes (one: 'un chicle' = 'a pack of gum').
- c02: Main weakness is monotony: whole units run on one frame ('X tiene N años', 'X es A, pero Y es B', '¿N pesos un caramelo?'). The new filler sentences are where the nonsense sits ('...pero mi hijo no', 'es de mis abuelos', 'Yo tengo un kiosco, vos no').
- c02: Numbers have a gap: 0-20, then 100-1000, with nothing for 21-99 in this stretch, so adults cannot say their age or a real price; 'cien' itself is never practised.
- c02: Unit titles and content drift after the reorder: 'Buy at the kiosco' is bakery food plus family plurals; 'Buy snacks by the dozen' is plurals and tu/mis; cuanto-sale carries 35 new forms while its neighbours carry 10.
- c02: Reality checks: coins ('monedas') are used to pay 500-1.200 pesos, which no longer happens; QR/transfer payment and the SUBE card are missing; Capital/provincia is used without explanation.
- c02: Words named in tips but never taught or drilled: 'afuera', 'cien', 'nuestra', 'sus', 'al'. Name swaps look fine here apart from a dog called Diego and a stray 'su novio'.
- c03: Spanish is clean: no grammar errors, voseo is consistent, no broken name swaps or gender clashes found in these 10 units.
- c03: Main weakness is content, not correctness: many filler lines with no real situation (pets that like eggs, 'Rocío camina', 'Necesito una palabra'), and unit 11 is almost 190 lines about 'palabra / castellano / inglés / celu'.
- c03: Some textbook wording remains where a porteño would say it differently: 'usar el castellano', 'caminar en el parque', calling 'Mozo,/Moza,' out loud, parents calling themselves 'los viejos de Pablo', 'a la noche' for night shifts.
- c03: Tips and sentences are sometimes out of step: 'cortado' appears in two tips but is not in the units, the 'más despacio' line is praised but never practised, the 'a la noche / de noche' tip contradicts the sentences, the gustar tip packs six rules.
- c03: Useful everyday items are missing: 'A mí también / tampoco', 'está frío', 'estoy mal', 'carne', 'plato'.
- c03: English is plain and good overall; small inconsistencies remain ('bar' as café or bar, 'mad' vs 'angry', 'picada' vs 'platter', 'Done.' for 'listo', 'you' vs 'you guys').
- c04: Spanish is clean and sounds porteño in almost every line (laburo, facu, depto, pieza, viejo, 'tengo médico', 'estudia para contador'); real errors are now rare — the worst are '¿De qué es tu laburo?', '¿Qué queremos para la picada?' and 'La psicóloga recibe en su casa'.
- c04: No name-swap damage found: genders, 'a + name' and English/Spanish names all match in these 9 units.
- c04: Main weakness is gaps between tips and sentences: things drilled but never explained (Con mi hermano vendemos = my brother and I, recibir = have guests, bus lines by number, los lunes, desde + present, hace…que) and things explained but never drilled (cuarto = fourth, desde/hasta opening hours).
- c04: la-hora is overloaded (35 words, three topics) and its two 'tarde' and two 'mañana' groups have sentences under the wrong meaning; a-que-hora-abre does not actually practise opening hours.
- c04: Drills lean on a few frames (¿Sos X? / Soy X, ¿Tiene X?, Son N mil pesos, tengo examen y estoy nerviosa) and the practice unit copies unit 20 sentences; the 'no un/una with jobs' rule is explained five times.
- c04: English is plain and readable, but inconsistent for the same word (kiosco/kiosk, university/college, salesperson/salesman, patio/courtyard) and simple present is sometimes used where '-ing' is needed.
- c05: Spanish is clean and properly porteño in all 14 units (laburo, bondi, celu, facu, pieza, hoy a la noche, tachero); I found almost no grammar errors and no broken name swaps.
- c05: Biggest problem is unit size and balance: queres-podes-vas (34 new words, 3 verbs + ir a + si) and ropa-y-colores (~280 very similar sentences, 4 dense tips) are overloaded; preferis-salir adds pensar/creer with no tip.
- c05: Tips and drills do not always match: en-taxi never practises 'Thames y Gorriti' or '¿Está libre?', tomar-el-bondi never practises 'hasta Palermo' / '¿Va a Palermo?', dale-veni never practises tené/decí/salí, 'llamá a una ambulancia' vs 'llamá una ambulancia'.
- c05: One real non-Argentine item: the 'ser listo = clever' example in the clothes tip (Spain usage).
- c05: English is plain and good, but one Spanish word often gets two English words (kiosco/kiosk, club/nightclub, roof/rooftop, produce store/fruit and vegetable shop, corner store/store), and 'car service' for remis reads badly.
- c05: Vocabulary gaps for real life: hostel basics (llave, cama, compartida), clothes beyond five items (buzo, camisa, pollera, talle), police report after a robbery.
- c06: Spanish is solid and sounds porteño: no tú/usted, no present perfect, good use of laburo, finde, depto, re, che, luca. I found no gender or name-swap breakage in these 11 units.
- c06: Main weakness is structure, not language: tips that pack 4-6 ideas (la-rutina, que-te-gusta-hacer), two overloaded routine units (35-40 new forms each), and things used before they are taught (le/les with gustar in unit 21, mucho before unit 25).
- c06: Headline phrases are under-practised or unexplained: 'te escribo' (unit 23), avisar (unit 21, no tip), 'get paid' sense of cobrar, 'me queda'. Key real-life lines are missing: 'Lo llevo', 'Solo estoy mirando', 'mandame un audio', '¡Qué lindo día!'.
- c06: English is mostly plain, but small inconsistencies remain: buzo = sweatshirt/hoodie, plaza = park once, cancha = stadium instead of game, facu = college, madrugar early/really early.
- c06: Two-speaker mini dialogues are written three different ways, most with no speaker marks, so they read as one person talking.
- c06: Peso prices and the dollar rate are written as exact numbers; they will go out of date and need a regular check.
- c07: Spanish is clean and sounds porteño overall; no name-swap gender errors found in these 8 units. English is accurate and plain.
- c07: Biggest real error: 'traer' is used where Argentines say 'llevar' (¿llevo hielo?, ¿qué llevo?) in about 10 sentences, and llevar is never taught.
- c07: Several tips are never practised: 'Hace = ago' (0 sentences), 'Te voy a esperar' pronoun placement (0 sentences), 'la semana que viene' in el-cumple, saber in the conocer/saber tip.
- c07: Reorder left el-cumple and ahora-y-planes back to back with the same grammar and tips; ahora-y-planes is overloaded (32 new words) and comi-y-sali mixes too many ideas.
- c07: Past-tense units are very repetitive and student-centred (examen, clase, facu, tarea, estudié mucho); adult-life situations (trámite, entrevista, turno, alquiler) are missing.
- c07: Small consistency issues: asado/kiosco/picada handled differently in the English, 'el finde' translated two ways, one unit uses a different dialogue format.
- c08: Spanish is clean and properly porteño across all ten units: no tú/usted, no present perfect, good use of re, finde, laburo, bondi, facu, viejos. I found no outright grammar errors.
- c08: Main weakness is repetition: the same few objects and frames carry whole units (la tarea x15, empanadas, paraguas, '—¿Dónde te duele? —En X', 'A [name] le duele X', '¿Pudiste ir a…?'). Adult, real-life variety is missing.
- c08: English is inconsistent for the same Spanish word: asado/barbecue, boliche club/nightclub, facu college/university/class, la Capital the city/Buenos Aires, plaza park/square. Dialogue dashes are also inconsistent.
- c08: Several tips need small fixes: the two el-finde tips contradict each other on 'entonces'; 'cenamos/salimos same in past and present' hides the -er exception; 'más buena' and buscar = pick up are used but never explained.
- c08: te-llamo is overloaded (direct + indirect pronouns + doubling + commands + se lo) while 'se lo' gets 3 sentences; vinieron-todos has 17 irregular forms at 4 sentences each.
- c08: Section 'What happened' now holds comparatives, health and pronouns after the reorder; health units lack basics like 'me siento mal', 'tengo tos', 'receta'.
- c09: Spanish is clean and sounds porteño almost everywhere; I found no grammar errors and no broken name swaps or gender clashes in these 10 units.
- c09: Biggest problem: three tips are contradicted by their own sentences (messages 'llegan' but the unit drills recibí; 'fui a' not 'viajé a' but 20 lines say viajé a; 'porteños say colegio' but the unit says escuela).
- c09: Heavy repetition: lavar los platos / sacar la basura in the chores units, and Mar del Plata / Bariloche / Mendoza + enero / julio in nearly every travel sentence.
- c09: Some new connector sentences (menos mal, por eso, por suerte, de repente) are fragments with no situation, and a few word blocks are pure filler (escuelas, escoba).
- c09: Gaps: everyday chores (hacer la cama, poner la mesa, pasar el trapo), story reactions (no te puedo creer, no sabés lo que me pasó), 'se lo' and 'e' before i- are used but not explained.
- c09: English is plain and good; only small inconsistencies (facu = college/university/class, plaza = park, kiosco left untranslated once).
- c10: Spanish quality is good: natural rioplatense, correct voseo, no tú/usted, no present perfect, almost no grammar errors found in about 1,000 sentences.
- c10: Structure is the main problem: units 9-14 (four football units, two food units) sit in the section "When I was a kid" and do not use the imperfect that units 1-8 just taught.
- c10: Football block is too long and too niche (about 55 words, many stadium-only), and it drills "hinchar por + club" in about 12 sentences against its own tips; "aguante" = "come on" and "popular" = "terrace" are misleading English.
- c10: Heavy repetition of a few props: fútbol/mate/abuela in the childhood units, "estaba lleno de gente" x11, superclásico x12, "árbitro no pega una" x5 (one exact duplicate), bife/flan/papas fritas as the only dishes.
- c10: Food units miss practical items a visitor needs (cubierto, "la cuenta, por favor", "una mesa para dos", fugazzeta/fainá, para llevar) and contain a logic slip ("muzza o vegetariana").
- c10: The scene/interruption units use the bare imperfect for actions in progress ("Caminaba cuando...") where porteños say "estaba caminando / iba caminando"; small English inconsistencies remain (asado/barbecue, plaza/park, as a girl/as a kid, choripán).
- c11: Spanish is clean and really porteño in this batch: almost no grammar errors, good use of tipo, en lo de, hacer dedo, invitó la picada, me dio bronca.
- c11: Main weakness is content, not language: repeated scenes (14 jealousy lines, 6 forgotten birthdays, Patagonia-en-auto, balcony toasts) and filler sentences in the travel units.
- c11: Several tips teach a phrase that no sentence practises (¿Cómo quedamos?, ya salgo, me dio miedo, tenemos la costumbre de, ruta 40).
- c11: Some units carry too many new forms at once (la-parrillada 14 meat words, llegue-tarde 17, me-olvide 29, acampamos 19) with only ~4 sentences per form.
- c11: A few cultural slips: ñoquis shown as Christmas food instead of the 29th; 'las fiestas' phrases people really use (pasar las fiestas, ¡feliz año!, se me pasó) are missing.
- c11: English is plain and good; only small issues ('got really happy', 'Tell about', mixed glosses for 'se me hizo tarde' and 'seguro').
- c12: Spanish is clean and porteño throughout: voseo right, good local words (flete, expensas, garantía, un toque, de paso, garrón), no tú/usted or Spain forms found.
- c12: Main weakness is boredom, not errors: same city names (Córdoba/Mendoza/Rosario/centro), same objects (sal, pan, agua, vaso) and copied sentences between neighbouring units.
- c12: Several units are overloaded (20, 20, 16, 15 new words) with only four sentences per word, while easy old words (necesito, bienvenida, cómodo) get long drills.
- c12: Tips and drills disagree in three places: 'acordarse de que', 'hablá más despacito', and 'dejen' (gloss says leave, sentences mean stop).
- c12: Title phrases not actually taught: '¿Me das una mano?', 'Están en su casa'; new words listed but not drilled: 'cuento', 'se casa'.
- c12: Gaps on the learner's side of the conversation: how to refuse a favour, what a guest says (permiso), positive 'hace falta', 'hacer acordar'.
- c13: Spanish is clean and sounds porteño almost everywhere; I found no real grammar errors, no tú/usted and no name-swap damage in these 10 units.
- c13: Main weakness is boredom: verdulería, el-celu and te-lo-devuelvo are built from a few frames with one noun swapped (¿Tenés X? / X, por favor / llaves and cargador in dozens of lines).
- c13: Several drill groups do not practise their unit: "fui" and "apagá" in the practice unit, "cocida" vegetables in the deli unit, "rompí" in the lending unit, "necesitar" barely used.
- c13: Order problems after the reorder: el-celu sits after the section's practice unit in "Plans and favours"; "está verde = unripe" is used one unit before it is explained; some drilled words (datos, wifi, app, verdulería, docena) are not in any NEW WORDS list here.
- c13: Theft content is useful but too long (two units in a row) and has unrealistic lines that point at a known thief ("Ese pibe es un chorro", "Ahí viene un motochorro"), while real emergency phrases (911, ¿estás bien?, necesito ayuda) are missing.
- c13: Tips are mostly accurate but several pack five or six points into one, and the le→se rule is repeated in four tips across two units; tips sometimes teach phrases that no sentence drills (te dejo, bajate, quedarse = stay, no me anda).
- c14: Spanish is clean and natural almost everywhere: real porteño lines (me cayó bárbaro, ¿dónde te diste la vacuna?, con este sueldo no llego). Very few true errors left.
- c14: Biggest problem: the two 'guessing' units teach that porteños say 'debe + infinitive', then drill dozens of future statements (Estará en el laburo, Estarás cansado, Diego no vendrá). Tips and sentences contradict each other.
- c14: Tips name things the sentences never practise: 'te lo presto / se lo digo' (0 sentences), 'debés' (0), 'naciste' (0), resfrío/remedio/guardia (almost 0).
- c14: Several units are overloaded (se-recibio 21 items, no-lo-aguanto 17, estamos-de-novios 17) so each word gets only 4 thin sentences, while filler words get 13 (descargá) or 16 (pastilla).
- c14: English is mostly plain, but a few idioms slip in (on/off the books, two-faced, laid off, ER, produce store) and the 'comes across to me' glosses for caer are not real English.
- c14: se-recibio mixes graduations, weddings and death in one unit, with a run of dead pets and a 'see you at the wake' line; it should be split.
- c15: Spanish is clean and natural almost everywhere: vos forms, laburo/bondi/finde, no tú/usted, no broken name swaps or gender clashes found in this batch.
- c15: Main weakness is sameness: section 8 drills (cuando-llegues, practice unit) are the same frame with one word changed, and the practice unit copies at least 8 sentences word-for-word from earlier units.
- c15: Tips promise more than the sentences practise: "Que te vaya bien" (the unit's own title phrase), igualmente, ¿cómo te fue?, te pido que, que yo sepa, seguro que, capaz que, the gracias-means-I'm-done mate rule.
- c15: Patterns are used before or without being explained: "no hace falta que" (9 uses before its tip), bare "que venga", "lo que quieras", "es mejor/difícil que", "no creo que" one unit early, "que no hiervas" in A2.
- c15: English is inconsistent rather than wrong: asado vs barbecue, "drink some mates", "share a mate with me", straw for bombilla, three different ways to mark two speakers.
- c15: The two mate units are good culturally, but te-convido-un-mate is overloaded (17 new words) while "pava" and amargo/dulce are missing.
- c16: Spanish is solid and clearly porteño across these 16 units; I found no broken name swaps or gender errors, only three 'a Ana' sound clashes.
- c16: Biggest structural problem: section 8 'Wishes and advice' ends with units on no sé / ¿qué significa? / lunfardo / goodbyes. The survival phrases (no entiendo, ¿me lo repetís?, ¿qué significa?) come far too late and are too easy for B1.1.
- c16: English for slang is uneven: chanta = 'fake/dishonest', besos = 'love/kisses', asado = 'barbecue' in five lines, kiosco vs kiosk. Needs one plain gloss per word.
- c16: Some units lean on one prop: flan about ten times in te-recomiendo, school homework/exams in no-se, bare 'Yo iría / Yo no iría' lines in yo-que-vos.
- c16: Review units copy old sentences word for word and review old words (conocí, me enojé, te acordás, 13 x estará) while new slang (embole, piola, cheto, berreta, fiaca) gets no review.
- c16: Tips are short and clear in general; three need fixing: novedades vs noticias (contradicted by the sentences), the bolded conditional endings, and the missing register warning for tu vieja / morfar / garpar.
- c17: The -ra/-ría core (units 3-4, 9) is solid: natural, short, good variety; few real errors there.
- c17: Tips and sentences contradict each other in three units: me-gustaria (tip says not for ordering, sentences order with it), usted (quisiera to order food), como-no-dona-rosa/preferiria (tip says 'permiso' and 'con gusto is formal', drills say the opposite).
- c17: Name swap damage is concentrated in the don/doña unit: 'Doña Cami', 'Don Nico', 'Mr. Diego'. Use older full names there.
- c17: After unit 9 the level drops to A2 (¿Tenés auto?, Llamé a mi mamá, Tengo veinte dólares) and the section title no longer fits; reorder left driving/money/news units too late and they never reuse the conditional.
- c17: Review units reuse lesson sentences word-for-word, and forced filler ('pedir permiso', 'va a llover', '¿Me prestás plata sin interés?') shows where the model padded word groups.
- c17: Real-life gaps: paying friends by transfer/alias, gas station, dólar blue, cortes/piquetes, chisme/chusmear.
- c18: Spanish is clean and mostly natural porteño; I found no grammar errors and no broken name swaps except the "a Ana" sound clash (3 lines).
- c18: Level is the main problem: buena-onda, es-medio-vago, donde-queda, zona-norte and por-las-dudas are A2 content (ser + adjective, ¿dónde queda?, llevá) sitting at B1.2 under a section called "If I were you".
- c18: Several units do not practise their own headline: "Ponele que sí", "Me dijo que no podía", "para que riegues/cierres", habíamos, and o sea/tipo/viste are named in summary or tip but not drilled.
- c18: Repetition by object or frame: "X es re Y" in buena-onda, celu/cargador/SUBE in se-me-cayo, 17 persiana lines in para-que-entres, "linda zona"; plus exact duplicate sentences in the practice unit.
- c18: Word choice against the unit's own advice: macanudo drilled though the tip calls it old-school; "se me acabó la nafta/batería" where porteños say "me quedé sin"; llevo glossed with the Spain meaning "I've been".
- c18: English is plain and accurate overall; small issues are "barbecue" vs house-rule "asado", stiff "answered that", and a few idioms (a real pain, plays dumb, a good sort).
- c19: Spanish is clean and natural almost everywhere: no tú/usted, good local words (bacha, canilla, calefón, alias, ojota), translations mostly faithful.
- c19: Tips and sentences contradict each other in several places: 'opino que', bare 'corrí'/'salté', 'se trabó el ascensor', plomero vs gasista.
- c19: Level drops sharply after the pluscuamperfecto unit: units 8–18 are A2-style word lists with present/preterite only, labelled B1.3.
- c19: Heavy repetition in small sets (transfer x42, complain x22, exaggerate x19) and practice units reuse the same sentences instead of mixing.
- c19: Section 'Stories and opinions' holds six repair/bank units that do not fit; word load per unit swings from 4 to 22.
- c19: English is inconsistent for asado/barbecue, kiosco/kiosk, sillón, plaza, and reclamar vs quejarse both = 'complained'.
- c20: Spanish is clean: almost no grammar errors or name/gender breaks from the name swap; most sentences are short, natural and usable.
- c20: Biggest problem: several units drill the textbook form their own tip says porteños avoid (acabar de vs recién, soler vs generalmente, de todos modos / sin embargo vs igual, llevar + gerund vs hace… que).
- c20: Reorder leftovers: volví a / vuelvo a and lo que are practised before the unit that introduces them; dejar de / volver a / fumar are taught three times with duplicate sentences.
- c20: Some units are overloaded (16-17 new words) or mix unrelated points (servir inside aunque), while the headline phrase or grammar of the unit is missing from the sentences (No sabés lo que me contaron, propina, querían que estudiara una carrera).
- c20: English is plain and good overall, but "asado" is "barbecue" in 13 lines against the house rule, plus a few idioms (Huh, got to work, pick this up) and stiff lines (for a short time).
- c20: Repetitive frames: "Mamá quería que…", English/German partner finds it weird, smoking, flu/fever, homework; dialogue punctuation is inconsistent.
- c21: Spanish is clean and Argentine almost everywhere; no broken name swaps or gender clashes found in these 11 units. The 'hacerse el', 'ni que', 'el de' and Migraciones content is useful and sounds real.
- c21: Biggest content problem: units contradict their own tips (por-un-lado drills 'opino que' after saying it is stiff; migraciones uses 'el colectivo demoró' after saying buses 'tardan').
- c21: Level is uneven for B2.1: color, elegir, decidir, no importa, donde and A2-size sentences ('Está vencido.', 'Elijo la roja.') sit next to the past-perfect subjunctive. The easy units never recycle the hard grammar.
- c21: Heavy repetition inside and across units: the same 4 adjectives for 'hacerse el', the same passport/form/queue lines, exact copies reused in review units, and many connector lines with no first half.
- c21: Tips are often overloaded or duplicated (two tips saying the same thing in el-que-quieras and el-de-la-vidriera; 5-topic tip in no-te-hagas-el-gil; dash-heavy tip in el-tramite). Missing notes: 'habría' as the twin of 'hubiera', 'should have' vs 'would have', indicative vs subjunctive after 'por más que'.
- c21: English is mostly plain and faithful; small issues are literal renderings ('the shameless guy', 'as if nothing had happened' for 'como si nada', 'you'd better' for 'te conviene') and three different dialogue formats.
- c22: Biggest issue: the units on advice and reported speech drill the textbook form (deberías, dijo que vendría/pondría) far more than the forms their own tips call the everyday porteño ones (tendrías que, yo que vos, iba a + infinitive, dijo que venía).
- c22: Spanish is clean: almost no grammar errors, no tú/usted, slang used well (clavó el visto, colgado, quemado, salir de garantía). The remaining Spanish problems are naturalness (demorar vs tardar, '¿debería…?' questions).
- c22: Repetition is the main quality drag: one frame repeated 8-15 times (cancelar, cambiar de idea, tenerte paciencia, dijo que vendría), and the practice unit copies six sentences word for word from earlier units.
- c22: Several units look too easy for B2: que-opinas (Estoy seguro. / No quiero discutir.) and deberias (Deberías dormir más.) are A2-B1 material; the B2 step (deberías haber…, no estoy tan seguro de que sea…) is missing.
- c22: Tips are mostly good and short, but many units carry two tips saying the same rule, and some glosses do not match what is drilled (puso, tenerte, cumplir, 'me pone nervioso' = nervous, nada que ver).
- c22: English is plain and accurate overall; leftovers are idioms in unit titles and a few lines (drives you up the wall, I'll give you that, bugs me, gets to me).
- c23: Spanish is clean and Argentine in almost every line; no name-swap or gender errors found in these 13 units.
- c23: Biggest problem: tips and drills disagree. The tip teaches one form (iba a, se puede, voy conociendo, justo hoy, salvo que lo necesite) and the drills practise another.
- c23: Level mismatch: sign units and price units are A2-B1 sentences ('El café subió', 'Leí el cartel') placed at B2.2, next to subjunctive conditions.
- c23: Padding: plural forms and plain verb forms count as 'new words', which creates many near-identical short drills (llegar a fin de mes x16, coffee prices x7, 'no choques' x9).
- c23: Slang units are fun and well warned (mina, pedo), but zarpado and dale que va have unclear or nonsense sentences, and the last unit crams 16 idioms.
- c23: Practice units copy sentences word for word instead of mixing the points; the second one reviews 'caer bien', which is not taught in its block.
- c24: Spanish is clean and natural almost everywhere; no real grammar errors found in ~1,000 lines. The problems are now about fit, not correctness.
- c24: Several units do not practise what their title/summary promise: 'Ask whether' has one 'me preguntó si' sentence, 'Ask for a raise' never asks, 'Fijate si' has one 'fijate', the email unit drills 'Estimado' although the tip calls it the exception.
- c24: The name swap left register clashes: 'Estimado Mati / Estimada Cami', 'Don Mati', 'doña Rocío' — nicknames where a formal or older-person name is needed.
- c24: Level mismatch in B2.2-B2.3: many groups are A2-B1 words and sentences (chiste, gracioso, link, archivo, fecha, negociar, disponible) padded with filler frames ('No sé negociar', 'Me encanta negociar').
- c24: English is mostly plain and faithful; weak spots are 'delivery' for a project deadline, 'Certainly' for cómo no, 'You believed it!' for ¡caíste!, the British title 'Fall for a wind-up', and 'barbecue' vs 'asado' inconsistency.
- c24: Tips are generally good and short; the exceptions are the over-long nickname/se calentó tip, the broken table in 'Si = whether', and the cryptic review tip about don José.
- c25: Spanish is clean: no real grammar errors, no name/gender breaks, no English-Spanish name mismatches found in these 14 units.
- c25: The 'la' idiom units (se la cree, me la jugué) are the best content here: real porteño speech. Problem is packing: 4 idioms in one unit, 10 in the next, and two tips that blur the idea (a la larga, la verdad).
- c25: Level is flat: units marked B2.3/B2.4 (ya no, cambió, edificio, largo/corto, por ejemplo, hay paro) are A2-B1 words in A2 sentences. Hard structures the tips mention (harto de que + subjunctive) are not practised.
- c25: Several textbook choices where a porteño says something else: acabar de (recién), soler, ya no (no ... más), me da estrés (me estresa), me despidieron (me echaron), más cercano, me saca de quicio (me saca).
- c25: Stories repeat the same few events: someone moved to Córdoba, someone quit, nobody found out; complaints rotate ruido/calor/subte. Practice units copy lesson sentences word for word instead of mixing items.
- c25: Some new-sentence filler built only to hold the target word ('una historia corta', 'el taxi más cercano', 'Pablo la tiene'); review tips are bare Spanish lists; 'demora' is announced and never used.
- c26: Spanish is clean and mostly natural; no real grammar errors found. The problems are now about fit, consistency and usefulness, not typos.
- c26: Tips and drills contradict each other in three places: levantar vs suspender el paro, "al parecer" in chat, and the single ballot vs old party-ballot sentences.
- c26: The passive units drill "fue + participle" in chat-style lines even though the tips say porteños do not talk like that; keep the passive inside news/headline frames.
- c26: For B2.4 the sentences are very short and beginner-level, and practice units copy many lines word for word from the units they review.
- c26: Section structure looks broken after the reorder: tango/music/football/parrilla units and a scams-and-repairs practice sit inside "The news and the street".
- c26: Two full units of first-person voting lines are of doubtful value for foreign learners; the tango unit misses what visitors need (dancing, classes, going to a milonga).
- c27: Spanish is clean and properly porteño almost everywhere (laburo, depto, bondi, finde, macana, a lo de); no tú/usted, no present perfect, no broken name swaps found. Only clash: 'a Ana' twice.
- c27: Biggest content problem: unit 17 drills formal 'rechazar una invitación' ~15 times while its own tip says people say 'decir que no'. Repetitive and not how porteños decline.
- c27: Pacing is very uneven: A2-easy 5-word units (lo bueno / lo malo) sit next to 15–16-item units (17, 21) and an 11-proverb unit. The two 'lo + adjective' units repeat the same city sentences.
- c27: Tips are often overloaded (5–10 expressions in one paragraph) or Spanglish with unglossed words; some teach phrases with no practice sentence in the unit (¡hubieras avisado!, ¡qué macana!, me entendiste mal).
- c27: English is mostly plain and faithful, but a few fixed translations need changing: 'There's no accounting for taste', 'it will cost you' for 'que le cueste', 'I have a commitment' vs 'I have plans' for the same sentence.
- c27: The practice unit copies sentences word for word and skips the main grammar it should review (tendría que haber, arrepentirse, no es que + quiera).
- c28: Spanish is clean: almost no grammar errors, names and genders are consistent after the swap, and the porteño expressions (me da cosa, me hace ruido, no me cierra, me cayó la ficha, sobre la hora, en negro) are well chosen and naturally used.
- c28: Biggest problem: the course drills words its own tips call bookish or written — 'tanto mejor' (19 sentences, the largest group in two units) and 'conservaron' — while the natural options (mejor todavía, mejor así, mantuvieron) get less practice.
- c28: Level mismatch: several B2.5 'Like a local' units teach A2/B1 words as new (canción, llorar, sonrisa, comedia, barco, italiano, perdoname); two full units cover the same reírse/llorar grammar, and the immigration unit is mostly 'X came by ship' repeated.
- c28: Tips and practice often do not match: phrases in tips with no sentences (careta, como dice el dicho, no fue mi intención, a medida que… te vas acostumbrando, mejor así) and drilled items missing from the tips (hacer la gamba, a la larga, ganar tiempo, se me partió el alma, helada).
- c28: English is mostly plain, but feelings are translated stiffly ('I felt fear / embarrassment / distress') and the same phrase gets different English in the teaching unit and its review unit (me hace ruido, no me cierra, no hay dos sin tres).
- c28: Practice units are very long (20–22 word groups) and reuse identical sentences from the taught units instead of recombining them.
- c29: Spanish is clean and natural overall: no grammar errors, no tú/usted, no broken name swaps or gender clashes found in these five units.
- c29: Main weakness is level: these are the last units of the course (B2.5) but most sentences are A2 shape (estar + adjective, 3-5 words). The ending feels easy and thin, especially the final unit.
- c29: Heavy overlap between units 18-20: emocionarse, discurso, corazón, me llegó are drilled, then drilled again in the practice unit with some identical sentences.
- c29: A few textbook choices where a porteño uses another word: me adapté a (me acostumbré), no logré dormir (no pude), mi apodo es (me dicen), se picó (se calentó/se ofendió), and 'me despido' drilled although the tip says not to use it.
- c29: Tips are friendly but uneven: one is only a word list, one packs six words, one gives a wrong rule (looking back = preterite), and some teach phrases that are never practised (¡grande!, los dejo, sos un orgullo).
- c29: English is plain and good; small slips only (barbecue instead of asado, 'got us emotional', 'what ravioli!', a few idioms in tips).
- c99: Structure is solid: every section mixes grammar, a real-life situation and local words, with review units; coverage for someone moving to Buenos Aires (SUBE, alias, expensas, obra social, paro, consorcio) is very good.
- c99: Main order problem: several basics come late - tens 21-99 after the hundreds, muy, ahora, hoy, ir, malo, este/eso, present of decir/ver/poner, 'hace' for time, reír/llorar, '¿dónde queda?', signs, DNI and residency paperwork.
- c99: Level labels drift at the top: many B2 units hold A2-B1 language (se alquila, ya no/cambió, tocás la guitarra, reír/llorar, cajero/alias), while A2 carries heavy niche blocks (four football units, irregular future).
- c99: Pacing is uneven: units run from 8 to 234 sentences and sections 3-4 are almost twice as heavy as section 8; section 1 front-loads long lists (countries, nationalities, family).
- c99: Every section break cuts a two-unit pair in half, so sections end and start off-topic; section 5's title covers only a third of its units.
- c99: Some themes return too often with little new (hearsay/gossip 8 units, counterfactual regrets 6, 'voy a' twice, transfers twice), while a few everyday things seem missing: dates, spelling an email, mío/tuyo, por/para, delivery.
